Open Question 4.14
.

The new notion of
lax interpretability
raises many questions. E.g.:
is there a good supremum for lax interpretability? And: does the embedding functor of interpretability into lax interpretability have a
right or left adjoint?
❍

4.2.
Essential Hereditary Undecidability meets Lax Interpretability

We start with the main insight concerning the relation between Essential Hereditary Undecidability and Lax Interpretability.

Theorem 4.15
.

Let
U
be RE and consistent.

i.

Suppose
\mathcal{V}
is a class of essentially undecidable theories and
U\mathrel{\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\blacktriangleright$}}}\mathcal{V}
.
Then,
U
is essentially undecidable.

ii.

Suppose
\mathcal{V}
is a class of essentially hereditarily undecidable theories and
U\mathrel{\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\blacktriangleright$}}}\mathcal{V}
.
Then,
U
is essentially hereditarily undecidable.

Proof.

Ad (i).
Suppose
\mathcal{V}
is a class of essentially undecidable theories and
U\mathrel{\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\blacktriangleright$}}}\mathcal{V}
.
Suppose
U
has a consistent decidable extension
W
. then,
W
has a decidable consistent
complete extension
W^{\ast}
. It follows that
W^{\ast}\rhd V
, for some
V
in
\mathcal{V}
.
Quod impossibile
.

Ad (ii).
Suppose
\mathcal{V}
is a class of essentially hereditarily undecidable theories and
U\mathrel{\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\blacktriangleright$}}}\mathcal{V}
.
Suppose
W
is an RE theory in the language of
U
and suppose
U^{\prime}:=U\cup W
is consistent.
We have to show that
W
is undecidable.

Let
U^{\prime\prime}
be a consistent RE extension of
U^{\prime}
such that
K:U^{\prime\prime}\rhd V
, for some
V\in\mathcal{V}
.
Consider the theory
Z:=\{\psi\mid W+\mathfrak{id}_{V}^{K}\vdash\psi^{K}\}
.
We have
Z\vdash\psi
iff
W+\mathfrak{id}_{V}^{K}\vdash\psi^{K}
.
Clearly
Z
is a sub-theory of
V
.
If
W
were decidable then so would
Z
, contradicting the fact that
V
is
essentially hereditarily undecidable.
9
9

9

We need small adaptations of the argument
in case we allow parameters. See also Footnote
6
.

❑

Remark 4.16
.

Inspection of the example provided by Ehrenfeucht in
[
Ehr61
]
, shows that his construction
provides an example where
U\mathrel{\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\blacktriangleright$}}}\mathcal{V}
, each element of
\mathcal{V}
is recursively inseparable (if we wish, even effectively inseparable),
but
U
is not recursively inseparable. The theory
U
of the example is essentially undecidable.
❍

We now turn to the result that motivates looking at classes of theories a relata of
\rhd
.
Let
{\mathfrak{S}}
be the set of all theories
\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}
, where
\sigma
is a false pure
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-sentence and
\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}
is consistent.
10
10

10

We can also
allow the inconsistent theory in
{\mathfrak{S}}
.

Theorem 4.17
.

We have
{\sf R}\mathrel{\gtriar\!\!\gtrial}{\mathfrak{S}}
.

Proof.

From left-to-right. Let
U^{\prime}
be a consistent RE extension of
R
. Clearly,
U^{\prime}\vdash\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}
, for all true pure
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-sentences

\sigma
. So, if no
\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{S}}
, would be consistent with
U^{\prime}
, we could decide
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-truth.
Quod non
.
Consider any such
\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}
that is consistent with
U^{\prime}
. Let
U^{\prime\prime}:=U^{\prime}+\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}
. Clearly,
U^{\prime\prime}\rhd\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}
.

From right-to-left. Consider
\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{S}}
. Clearly,
\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}\rhd{\sf R}
and we are easily done.
❑

Of course, the extension
U^{\prime\prime}
in the proof of Theorem
4.17
can be found effectively from an index of
U^{\prime}
.
We outline one way to do it.

Sketch of an alternative proof of Theorem
4.17
.

Let
\necessary
be
U^{\prime}
-provability. By the Gödel Fixed Point Lemma, we can find a pure
\Sigma_{1}
-sentence
\jmath
that is equivalent to
\necessary\neg\,\jmath^{\sf q}
.
11
11

11

We
need a bit of careful attention to ensure that our sentence is pure.

Suppose
\jmath
were true, then we have both
\necessary\jmath^{\sf q}
and
\necessary\neg\,\jmath^{\sf q}
, contradicting the consistency of

U^{\prime}
. So
\jmath
is false and
\jmath^{\sf q}
is consistent with
U^{\prime}
. We take
U^{\prime\prime}:=U^{\prime}+\jmath^{\sf q}
.
❑

Since all
\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{S}}
extend
R
, they are essentially undecidable. Moreover, since the
\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}
are finitely axiomatised, they
are essentially hereditarily undecidable. It follows from Theorem
4.15
, that
R
is essentially hereditarily undecidable. So, this gives us a proof of Theorem
3.11
.

Open Question 4.18
.

Suppose
U\mathrel{\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\blacktriangleright$}}}{\mathfrak{S}}
. Does it follow that
U
is recursively inseparable?
❍

Let
{\mathfrak{F}}
be the set of all finitely axiomatised essentially hereditarily undecidable theories. Example
3.12
shows that
there is an essentially hereditarily undecidable theory
U
such that
U\not\mathrel{\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\blacktriangleright$}}}{\mathfrak{F}}
.

5.
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-friendliness and
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-representativity

In this section, we have a brief look at a rather natural property of theories that implies essential hereditary undecidability.

Consider a consistent RE theory
U
and a
recursive function
\varPhi
from pure 1-
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-sentences to
U
-sentences. We give three possible properties of

\varPhi
. Let
\sigma
range over pure 1-
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-sentences.

\Sigma
1.

If
\sigma
is true, then
U\vdash\varPhi(\sigma)
.

\Sigma
2.

(U+\varPhi(\sigma))\rhd\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}
.

\Sigma
3.

Suppose
\sigma\leq\sigma^{\prime}
. Then,
U\vdash\neg\,\varPhi(\sigma^{\prime}<\sigma)
.
Similarly, suppose
\sigma<\sigma^{\prime}
. Then,
U\vdash\neg\,\varPhi(\sigma^{\prime}\leq\sigma)
.

We say that
U
is
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-friendly
iff, there is a recursive
\varPhi
satisfying
\Sigma 1
and

\Sigma 2
. We say that
U
is
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-representative
if there is a recursive
\varPhi
satisfying
\Sigma 1
and

\Sigma 3
.

The next theorem is, in a sense, a generalisation of the First Incompleteness Theorem. We just need
\Sigma 1
.

Theorem 5.1
.

Consider a consistent RE theory
U
and a
recursive function
\varPhi
from pure 1-
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-sentences to
U
-sentences.
Suppose
\varPhi
satisfies
\Sigma 1
. Let
U_{i}
be a recursive sequence of
consistent RE extensions of
U
. Then, we can effectively find a false pure 1-
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-sentence
\jmath
, such that

\varPhi(\jmath)
is consistent with each of the
U_{i}
.

Proof.

We stipulate the conditions of the theorem.
We can find a pure
\Delta_{0}
-formula
\pi(i,p,\varphi)
such that
U_{i}\vdash\varphi
iff
\exists p\;\pi(i,p,\varphi)
.
Let

{\vartriangle}\varphi:=\exists u\,\exists i\mathbin{<}u\,\exists p\mathbin{<}u\;\,\pi(i,p,\varphi).

Using the Gödel Fixed Point Construction, we find a pure 1-
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-sentence
\jmath
such that
\jmath
is true
iff
{\vartriangle}\neg\,\varPhi(\jmath)
.
Suppose
\jmath
is true.
Then,
U\vdash\varPhi(\jmath)
and, for some
i
, we have
U_{i}\vdash\neg\,\varPhi(\jmath)
,
contradicting the consistency of
U_{i}
. Thus,
\jmath
is false and consistent with each of the
U_{i}
.
❑

Remark 5.2
.

(Kripke).
We immediately get Kripke’s version of the First Incompleteness Theorem from Theorem
5.1
.
Let
School
be the theory in the language of arithmetic (without
<
as primitive) of all true closed equations. We get Kripke’s result by
setting
U:={\sf School}
and
\varPhi
the transformation promised by Matiyasevich’s theorem that sends a pure 1-
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-sentence
to a purely existential sentence.

E.g., it follows that there is a Diophantine equation that has solutions in all finite rings and in some non-standard model of
PA
, but no
solutions in
\mathbb{N}
.
❍

Lemma 5.1
.

Every
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-friendly theory
U
is
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-representative.

Proof.

Let
\varPhi
witness that
U
is
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-friendly. We prove
\Sigma 3
. Let
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
be pure 1-
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-sentences.
We have:

(U+\varPhi(\sigma^{\prime}<\sigma))\rhd[\sigma^{\prime}<\sigma]
. Suppose
\sigma\leq\sigma^{\prime}
. Since, by Theorem
2.4
,

[\sigma^{\prime}<\sigma]
is inconsistent, we find that
U\vdash\neg\,\varPhi(\sigma^{\prime}<\sigma)
.
The other case is similar.
❑

Theorem 5.3
.

Suppose
U
is RE, consistent, and
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-friendly. Then,
U\mathrel{\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\blacktriangleright$}}}{\mathfrak{S}}
, and, hence,
U\mathrel{\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\blacktriangleright$}}}{\sf R}
.

Proof.

We note that any consistent RE extension of a
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-friendly RE theory is again
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-friendly. So it is sufficient to show that

U
tolerates a theory
\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}
, for false pure 1-
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-sentences
\sigma
.

Suppose
U
does not tolerate any false
\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}
. If
\sigma
is true, we have
U\vdash\varPhi(\sigma)
.
Suppose
\sigma
is false. We have
(U+\varPhi(\sigma))\rhd\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}
. So, if
U+\varPhi(\sigma)
were consistent,
then
U
would tolerate
\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}
.
Quod non, ex hypothesi
. So,
U\vdash\neg\,\varPhi(\sigma)
.
Since
U
is RE, we can now decide the halting problem.
Quod impossibile
.
❑

We note that we can effectively find a sentence
\sigma
such that
\varPhi(\sigma)
is consistent with
U
from indices for

U
and
\varPhi
. Let
\necessary\neg\,\varPhi(s)
be a pure 1-
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-formula representing the
U
-provability of
\neg\,\varPhi(s)
.
Then, we can take
\sigma
to be
\jmath
, (a pure 1-
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
version of) the Gödel fixed point that is equivalent to

\necessary\neg\,\varPhi(\jmath)
. It is easy to see that
U+\varPhi(\jmath)
is indeed consistent.

The following result employs the notions and notations of
[
Vis22
]
.

Theorem 5.4
.

Every consistent RE effectively Friedman-reflexive theory
U
is
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-friendly.

Proof.

We can take
\varPhi(\sigma):=\xpossible\sigma

❑

We note that Theorems
5.3
and
5.4
immediately give Theorem
3.13
.

The theory
R
is
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-friendly via the mapping
\sigma\mapsto\bigwedge\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}
. So this again shows that
{\sf R}\mathrel{\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\blacktriangleright$}}}{\mathfrak{S}}
.

It turns out that
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-representativity coincides with a familiar notion.

Theorem 5.5
.

Consider a consistent RE theory
U
. Then,
U
is
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-representative iff
U
is effectively inseparable.

Proof.

Suppose
U
is RE and consistent.

Suppose
U
is
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-representative as witnessed by
\varPsi
. Let
\mathcal{X}_{0}
and
\mathcal{X}_{1}
be any pair of effectively inseparable sets.
Let
\sigma_{0}(x)
be a pure 1-
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-formula that represents
\mathcal{X}_{0}
and
let
\sigma_{1}(x)
be a pure 1-
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-formula that represents
\mathcal{X}_{1}
.
We write
\sigma_{i}(\underline{n})
for a pure 1-representation of the result of substituting
\underline{n}
in
\sigma_{i}
.
We define

\varTheta(n):=\varPsi(\sigma_{0}(\underline{n})\leq\sigma_{1}(\underline{n})).

Suppose
n\in\mathcal{X}_{0}
. Then,
\sigma_{0}(\underline{n})\leq\sigma_{1}(\underline{n})
is true
and, hence,
U\vdash\varTheta(n)
. Suppose
n\in\mathcal{X}_{1}
. Then,

\sigma_{1}(\underline{n})<\sigma_{0}(\underline{n})
is true, and, hence,

U\vdash\neg\,\varTheta(n)
.

For the converse, suppose
U
is effectively inseparable. Then, by
[
BPEK67
, Theorem 2]
, we find that there is a recursive boolean isomorphism
\varPsi
from

R
to
U
. We can take
\varPsi
restricted to pure 1-
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-sentences
as the function witnessing the
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-representativity of
U
.
❑

The first part of the proof of Theorem
5.5
can also be done via a Rosser argument. We have to be somewhat more careful
with the details if we follow that road. We will give the argument in Appendix
B
.

Open Question 5.6
.

It would be quite interesting to replace the
\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}
in our definitions of friendliness and representativity by some other class of theories. However, the demands on
the
\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}
use
both witness comparison and truth. So, it is not at all obvious here what more general analogues could be.
❍

Example 5.7
.

At this point the time is ripe to give some separating examples. We consider properties:
P

P
2: essentially undecidable,
P
3: essentially hereditarily undecidable,

P
4: recursively inseparable,
P
5: effectively inseparable,
P
6:
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-friendly. We first give the list and then the description of
the examples below it.

example

a.

We can take

be any decidable theory like Presburger Arithmetic.

b.

We can take

e.g. the theory of groups, which, by results of Tarski (

We can take

to be a finitely axiomatised theory that is recursively boolean isomorphic to

. This theory is essentially hereditarily undecidable, but

Let

be an RE Turing degree with

.
Suppose
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
is a recursively inseparable pair of RE sets as constructed by Shoenfield (see
[
Sho58
]
or
[
PMV22
]
), where the
Turing degree of
\mathcal{A}
is
d
and the Turing degree of
\mathcal{B}
is
\leq d
. Let

U_{4}:={\sf Jan}+\{{\sf A}_{n}\mid n\in\mathcal{A}\}+\{\neg\,{\sf A}_{n}\mid n\in\mathcal{B}\}.

Then,
U_{4}
is recursively inseparable, but cannot be effectively inseparable. Also, since
U_{4}
contains a decidable sub-theory in the
same language it cannot be essentially hereditarily undecidable.

f.

We define
U_{5}
like
U_{4}
only now we take
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
to be effectively inseparable.

g.

We can take
U_{6}
to be the theory of Hanf’s example (Example
3.12
in this paper) for the case that the recursively inseparable sets on which the construction is based
are effectively inseparable.

h.

We can take
U_{7}
to be, e.g.,
Q
.

We note that our list shows that the evident dependencies of the concepts are all possible dependencies.
❍

Open Question 5.8
.

Is there a finitely axiomatised and effectively inseparable theory that is not
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-friendly?
❍

6.
Separating Model-Interpretability and Lax Interpretability

In this section, we introduce the theory
{{\sf PA}^{-}_{\sf scat}}
and prove some of its salient properties.
Most importantly, it will be an example of a
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-friendly theory
U
such that
U
is a sub-theory of
R
and
U\mathrel{\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\blacktriangleright$}}}{\sf R}
, and
U\mathrel{\not\!\rhd}_{\sf mod}{\sf R}
.

Remark 6.1
.

Our theory
{{\sf PA}^{-}_{\sf scat}}
is closely related to Vaught’s theory
S
. See
[
Vau62
]
.
❍

We define the theory
{\sf PA}^{-}_{\sf scat}
as follows. It has the relational signature of arithmetic with
<
minus the zero.
We write
\widetilde{n}(a)
for
(\exists z\,z=\underline{n})^{\mathfrak{q}}
with 0 replaced by the parameter
a
plus ‘there are at least
n
elements’ relativised to
the domain of the
y
such that
a\leq y
.
We note that number and theory fix each other uniquely.

{\sf PA}^{-}_{\sf scat}
is axiomatised by the axioms
\exists a\;\widetilde{n}(a)
for
n\in\omega
.

Let
{\sf R}_{\sf succ}
be the theory in the language with 0 and
S
, axiomatised by
\underline{n}\neq\underline{m}
, where
n\neq m
.
We prove the following theorem.

Theorem 6.2
.

We have:

a.

{\sf R}\supseteq_{\sf e}{{\sf PA}^{-}_{\sf scat}}
.

b.

{{\sf PA}^{-}_{\sf scat}}
is
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-friendly and, hence,
{\sf PA}^{-}_{\sf scat}\mathrel{\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\blacktriangleright$}}}{\sf R}
.

c.

{\sf PA}^{-}_{\sf scat}\mathrel{\not\!\rhd}_{\sf mod}{\sf R}_{\sf succ}
.

We note that it follows that
{{\sf PA}^{-}_{\sf scat}}\mathrel{\gtriar\!\!\otrial}{\sf R}
, and, hence,
{{\sf PA}^{-}_{\sf scat}}\mathrel{\gtriar\!\!\gtrial}{\sf R}
.

We prove our theorem via a sequence of lemmas.
The following lemma is clear.

Lemma 6.1
.

{\sf R}\supseteq_{\sf e}{{\sf PA}^{-}_{\sf scat}}
.

For any pure 1-
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-sentence,
\sigma
we
define
\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}_{x}
as the theory of
\sigma
with zero replaced by the free parameter
x
on the domain of the
y
such that
x\leq y
. We show the following lemma.

Lemma 6.2
.

The theory
{\sf PA}^{-}_{\sf scat}
is
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-friendly. Hence,
{\sf PA}^{-}_{\sf scat}\mathrel{\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\blacktriangleright$}}}{\mathfrak{S}}
and
{\sf PA}^{-}_{\sf scat}\mathrel{\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\blacktriangleright$}}}{\sf R}
.

Proof.

It is easy to see that the mapping
\sigma\mapsto\exists x\,\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}_{x}
fulfils the conditions for
\varPhi
in the definition of
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-friendlyness.
12
12

12

This uses
interpretations with parameters. At the end of the section, we explain how to obtain the result, for a variant of

{\sf PA}^{-}_{\sf scat}
, in a parameter-free way. Here we loose the fact that our theory is contained in
{\sf R}_{<}
, but we still have that
R
interprets it.

❑

To prove Theorem
6.2
(c), we need a counter-model.
We define
\mathbb{N}_{\sf scat}
, the model of the scattered numbers, for the signature of
{\sf PA}^{-}_{\sf scat}
as follows.
It is the disjoint sum of the natural numbers considered as models of the theory of a number (in relational signature).
Modulo isomorphism, we can also define
\mathbb{N}_{\sf scat}
concretely as follows.

\bullet

The domain of
\mathbb{N}_{\sf scat}
is the set of
{\langle n,m\rangle}
with
m<n
.

\bullet

{\langle n,m\rangle}<{\langle n^{\prime},m^{\prime}\rangle}
iff
n=n^{\prime}
and
m<m^{\prime}
.

\bullet

{\sf S}({\langle n,m\rangle},{\langle n^{\prime},m^{\prime}\rangle})
iff
n=n^{\prime}
and
m^{\prime}={\sf min}(m+1,n-1)
.

\bullet

{\sf A}({\langle n,m\rangle},{\langle n^{\prime},m^{\prime}\rangle},{\langle n^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}\rangle})
iff
n=n^{\prime}=n^{\prime\prime}
and
m^{\prime\prime}={\sf min}(m+m^{\prime},n-1)
.

\bullet

{\sf M}({\langle n,m\rangle},{\langle n^{\prime},m^{\prime}\rangle},{\langle n^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}\rangle})
iff
n=n^{\prime}=n^{\prime\prime}
and
m^{\prime\prime}={\sf min}(m\times m^{\prime},n-1)
.

\bullet

{\langle n,m\rangle}<{\langle n^{\prime},m^{\prime}\rangle}
iff
n=n^{\prime}
and
m<m^{\prime}
.

Let
\sim
be the equivalence relation on
\mathbb{N}_{\sf scat}
given by
x<y\vee y\leq x
.
We write
\lceil x\rceil
for (the purely syntactic representation of) the
\sim
-equivalence class of
x
. Let
\varphi^{\lceil x\rceil}
be the relativisation of the
quantifiers of
\varphi
to
\lceil x\rceil
.
13
13

13

To be precise: we first rename the variables in
\varphi
so that there
is no bound variable labeled
x
and then relativise.

We write
{\hat{n}}
for the submodel (of the theory of
n
) with as domain the
\sim
-equivalence class of size
n
.

Consider a finite set of free variables
X
. Let
\simeq
be any equivalence relation on
X
. So,
\simeq
is a purely syntactic
relation.
We say that a formula is
\theta
is
\simeq
-good
if it is of the form
\chi^{\lceil x\rceil}
, for some
x\in X
and all free variables of

\theta
are
\simeq
-equivalent to
x
. We say that a formula is
\simeq
-friendly
iff it is a boolean combination of
\simeq
-good formulas
of the form
\chi^{\lceil x\rceil}
, where
x\in X
.
We define
{\sf E}_{\simeq}
to be the conjunction of all
x\sim x^{\prime}
in case
x\simeq x^{\prime}

and
x\not\sim x^{\prime}
in case
x\not\simeq x^{\prime}
, for
x,x^{\prime}\in X
. (We assume that
X
is part of the data for
\simeq
.)

We prove our third lemma. The lemma and its proof are well known for the case of binary disjoint sums of models.
The present result is just an adaptation.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
X
be some finite set of variables and let

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle x\hfil

be some enumeration of
X
.
Consider a formula
\varphi
with free variables among
X
and an equivalence relation
\simeq
on
X
.
Then, there is an
\simeq
-friendly
\psi
such that

\mathbb{N}_{\sf scat}\models\forall\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}\,({\sf E}_{\simeq}\to(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)).

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
\varphi
.

We treat
{\sf A}xyz
as a prototypical atomic formula, where
A
stands for addition.
In case
{\sf A}xyz
is
\simeq
-good, we can take
{\sf A}xyz
itself as
\psi
noting that

{(\sf A}xyz)^{\lceil x\rceil}
is identical to
{\sf A}xyz
. In case
{\sf A}xyz
is not
\simeq
-good, we can take
\psi
to be

\bot
, seeing that
{\sf A}xyz
is equivalent to
\bot
under the assumption
{\sf E}_{\simeq}
and
\bot=\bot^{\lceil x\rceil}
.

We treat
(\varphi_{0}\wedge\varphi_{1})
as a prototypical case.
In
{\sf Th}(\mathbb{N}_{\sf scat})
, under the assumption
{\sf E}_{\simeq}
, by the Induction Hypothesis,
each of the
\varphi_{i}
is equivalent to an
\simeq
-friendly
\psi_{i}
. So,

(\varphi_{0}\wedge\varphi_{1})
is equivalent to

(\psi_{0}\wedge\psi_{1})
, which is
\simeq
-friendly.

Finally, consider the case of
\exists z\,\varphi^{\prime}
.
Since we always can rename bound variables, we can assume that
z\not\in X
.
Let
X^{\prime}:=X\cup\{z\}
. We write
{\simeq}\sqsubset_{z}{\simeq^{\prime}}
if
\simeq^{\prime}
is an
equivalence relation on
X^{\prime}
and
\simeq^{\prime}
restricted to
X
is
\simeq
.

We reason in
{\sf Th}(\mathbb{N}_{\sf scat})
under the assumption
{\sf E}_{\simeq}
.
We note that
\exists z\,\varphi^{\prime}
is equivalent to
\bigvee_{{\simeq}\sqsubset_{z}{\simeq^{\prime}}}\exists z\,({\sf E}_{\simeq^{\prime}}\wedge\varphi^{\prime})
.
We zoom in on some
\alpha:=({\sf E}_{\simeq^{\prime}}\wedge\varphi^{\prime})
. By the induction hypothesis, this can be rewritten
as the conjunction of
{\sf E}_{\simeq^{\prime}}
and a disjunction of formulas
of the form
(\theta_{0}\wedge\theta_{1})
, where
\theta_{0}
is
\simeq
-friendly and

\theta_{1}
is
\simeq^{\prime}
-good and of the form
\chi^{\lceil y\rceil}
, where
y\simeq z
. If the
\simeq^{\prime}
-equivalence class of

z
contains at least two elements, we choose
y
different from
z
.
Given our assumption that
{\sf E}_{\simeq}
, the formula

\alpha
is equivalent to a disjunction of formulas of the form
(\theta_{0}\wedge({\sf E}_{\simeq^{\prime}}\wedge\theta_{1}))
.
It follows that
\exists z\ \alpha
is equivalent to
a disjunction of formulas of the form
(\theta_{0}\wedge\exists z\,({\sf E}_{\simeq^{\prime}}\wedge\theta_{1}))
.
It clearly suffices to show that
\exists z\,({\sf E}_{\simeq^{\prime}}\wedge\theta_{1})
is equivalent to
an
\simeq
-friendly formula.

There are two cases. The
\simeq^{\prime}
-equivalence class of
z
contains at least two elements or precisely one.

In the first case we can replace
\exists z\,({\sf E}_{\simeq^{\prime}}\wedge\theta_{1})
by

\exists z\in\lceil y\rceil\,\theta_{1}
, where
\theta_{1}
is of the form
\chi^{\lceil y\rceil}
. Clearly,
\exists z\in\lceil y\rceil\,\theta_{1}
is
\simeq
-good.

In the second case, at most
z
occurs freely in
\theta_{1}
.
Suppose
X=\{x_{0},\dots,x_{k-1}\}
. Let
\theta_{1}
be
\chi^{\lceil z\rceil}
.
In the context of
{\sf E}_{\simeq}
, we can rewrite
\exists z\,({\sf E}_{\simeq^{\prime}}\wedge\theta_{1})
to the formula

\exists z\,(\bigwedge_{i<k}z\not\sim x_{i}\wedge\theta_{1})
.
In case
\chi^{\lceil z\rceil}
can be
fulfilled in more than
k
submodels corresponding to numbers,
\exists z\,(\bigwedge_{i<k}z\not\sim x_{i}\wedge\theta_{1})

will be true. So we can replace
\exists z\,(\bigwedge_{i<k}z\not\sim x_{i}\wedge\theta_{1})

by
\top
. If not
\chi(z)
will be fulfilled in precisely
{\hat{n}}_{0},\dots,{\hat{n}}_{s-1}
.
Let

{\mathfrak{c}}_{n}(u):=\exists v_{0}\dots\exists v_{n-1}\,(\bigwedge_{i<j<n}v_{i}\neq v_{j}\wedge\forall w\,(w=u\leftrightarrow\bigvee_{i<n}w=v_{i})).

We find that
\exists z\,(\bigwedge_{i<k}z\not\sim x_{i}\wedge\theta_{1})
is equivalent to

\bigvee_{j<s}\bigwedge_{i<k}\neg\,{\mathfrak{c}}^{\lceil x_{i}\rceil}_{n_{j}}(x_{i})
, which is clearly
\simeq
-friendly.
14
14

14

Note that
if
s=0
, this formula becomes
\bot
.

❑

Lemma 6.4
.

There is no inner model of
{\sf R}_{\sf succ}
in
\mathbb{N}_{\sf scat}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbb{N}_{\sf scat}
has at least two elements, we do not need to consider piece-wise interpretations. Moreover,
every element in
\mathbb{N}_{\sf scat}
is definable. So, we can always eliminate parameters. Thus it is sufficient to prove
our result for many-dimensional relativised interpretations without parameters.

Suppose we had an inner model of
{\sf R}_{\sf succ}
given by an interpretion
M
. Say
M
is
m
-dimensional
and suppose
0
is given by a formula
{\mathfrak{z}}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern . We note that in the sequence
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern -good, say, it is of the form

Consider any
\theta_{qp}
where
w_{qp}
is one of the
y_{i}
. There are two possibilities.

I.

Suppose the number of
{\hat{n}}
in which
\chi_{qp}
is satisfiable is

<2m+1
. In this case we add all
n
such that
\chi_{qp}
is satisfiable in
{\hat{n}}
to
\mathcal{N}
.

II.

Suppose the number of
{\hat{n}}
in which
\chi_{qp}
is satisfiable is

\geq 2m+1
. In this case we add the first
2m+1
such
n
to
\mathcal{N}
.

iii.

Nothing more will be in
\mathcal{N}
.

Let
\mathcal{N}^{\ast}
be the elements in the
{\hat{n}}
, for
n\in\mathcal{N}
.
Clearly
\mathcal{N}
is finite and so is the number of elements in
\mathcal{N}^{\ast}
.

We are now ready and set to define our infinite sequence in order to obtain the desired contradiction.
The sequence starts with

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

. We note that

and that the components of

.

Consider the variable
w_{qp}
. If it is an
x_{i}
, then all variables
y_{i}
that are
\simeq
-equivalent to it, will have values that are

\sim
-equivalent to
b_{i}
. So they will be in
\mathcal{N}^{\ast}
. If it is a
y_{i}
and we are in case
(ii.I) of the construction of
\mathcal{N}
the values of the variables equivalent to it
will be in
\mathcal{N}^{\ast}
.
The final case is that
w_{qp}
is a
y_{i}
and we are in case (ii.II) of the construction of
\mathcal{N}
.
Since there are at least
2m+1
numbers
n
such that
{\hat{n}}
satisfies
\chi_{qp}
, we can always choose
an
n^{\ast}
among these numbers such that no
d_{i}
is in
{\hat{n}}^{\ast}
such that
{\hat{n}}^{\ast}
satisfies
\theta_{qp}
.
We assign to
y_{i}
in the equivalence class of
w_{qp}
the value
e_{i}
so that under this assignment
\chi_{q,p}
is satisfied.
We now modify our sequence

. We note that the new sequence has strictly less

\mathcal{N}^{\ast}
and that we still have
{\mathfrak{s}}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle c^{\prime}\hfil$\crcr}}}\,)
.
We repeat this procedure for all
w_{qp}
that are among the
y_{i}
. The final sequence we obtain will only have values in
\mathcal{N}^{\ast}
.

By the axioms of
{\sf R}_{\sf succ}
, we we cannot have two elements in our sequence that are the same. A contradiction.
❑

We end this section by describing how we can make the result work for parameter-free interpretations.
A first step is to modify the definition of
\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}
, say, to
\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}\ast}
. We remind the reader that we assume our

\sigma
are in pure form. In the definition of
\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}
we just asked for there to be a witness of
\sigma
.
For
\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}\ast}
we ask that the witness
w
is the smallest one and that
w+1
is the maximum element.

We now define
{\sf PA}^{-}_{\sf scat}!
as the theory axiomatised by
\exists!x\,\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}\ast}(x)
.
We note that
\mathbb{N}_{\sf scat}
also satisfies
{\sf PA}^{-}_{\sf scat}!
.
The new theory is not a sub-theory of
{\sf R}_{<}
. However, the theory is locally finite, i.e., every finitely axiomatised sub-theory has a finite model.
So, by the main result of
[
Vis14b
]
, we have
{\sf R}\rhd{\sf PA}^{-}_{\sf scat}!
.
15
15

15

We can also provide an interpretation is a
more direct way by mimicking the definition of
\mathbb{N}_{\sf scat}
in
R
.

All our other arguments work with
\exists!x\,\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}\ast}(x)
replacing
\exists x\,\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}}(x)
.
We note that
{\sf PA}^{-}_{\sf scat}!+\exists!x\,\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}\ast}(x)
interprets
\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}\ast}
in a parameter-free way.
E.g., the definition of the domain becomes:
\delta(y):=\exists x\,(\sigma^{\mathfrak{q}\ast}(x)\wedge x\leq y)
.

7.
Non-Minimality

There is no interpretability minimal essentially hereditarily undecidable theory. There is a quick proof of this due to Fedor Pakhomov and there is
a slow proof. Since, the slow proof yields different information, I do reproduce it here. See also Remark
7.3
.

Here is the quick proof.
The proof is a minor adaptation of the proof of
[
PMV22
, Theorem 1.1]
as given in Section 4.4 of that paper.

Theorem 7.1
.

There is no interpretability minimal essentially hereditarily undecidable RE theory.

Proof.

Since, by Theorem
3.3
, the essentially hereditarily undecidable theories are closed under interpretability suprema, it is sufficient to show that
there is no
minimum
essentially hereditarily undecidable RE theory. Suppose, to obtain a contradiction, that

U^{\star}
is such a minimum theory.

Let
i
be an index of an RE set. By a result of Shoenfield
[
Sho58
]
, we can effectively find an index
j
of the theory
{\sf sh}(i)
such that

{\sf W}_{i}
is not recursive iff
{\sf sh}(i)
is essentially undecidable.
See also
[
PMV22
, Theorem 4.8]
. Next, by a result of Peretyat’kin
[
Per97
]
,
we can effectively find an index
k
of a theory
{\sf pere}(j)
that is finitely axiomatized and recursively Boolean isomorphic with

{\sf sh}(i)
.
Let us call this theory

{\sf shpe}(i)
. Since
{\sf shpe}(i)
is essentially undecidable and finitely axiomatized it is essentially hereditarily undecidable.
Let
Rec
be the set of indices of recursive sets. We have:

\displaystyle i\not\in{\sf Rec}

iff

\displaystyle{\sf shpe}(i)\text{ is hereditarily essentially undecidable}

iff

\displaystyle{\sf shpe}(i)\rhd U^{\star}.

By a result of Rogers and, independently, Mostowski,
Rec
is complete

\Sigma^{0}_{3}
. See
[
Rog67
, Chapter 14, Theorem XVI]
or
[
Soa16
, Corollary 4.3.6]
.
We have reduced the complement of
Rec
, a
\Pi^{0}_{3}
-complete predicate, to an
interpretability statement: a
\Sigma^{0}_{3}
-predicate.
Quod impossibile
.
❑

We prove the non-minimality result w.r.t. interpretability for essentially hereditarily undecidable theories again using the idea behind the construction from the
proof of
[
Han65
, Theorem 3.2 ]
, following the plan of the proof of
[
PMV22
, Theorem 1.1.]
as given in Section 4.2 of that paper.

We will need a variation on Kleene’s well known construction of two effectively inseparable sets.
We write
x\cdot y
for Kleene application. For
i=0,1
,
let
{\sf Km}_{i}:=\{{\langle n,x\rangle}\mid x\cdot{\langle n,x\rangle}\simeq i\}
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Suppose
\mathcal{W}
is a recursive set. Let
\varTheta
be a 0,1-valued recursive function such that
\varTheta(x)=1
iff
x\in\mathcal{W}
.
We can find an index
c
of
\varTheta
effectively from an index
i
of
\mathcal{W}
.
Then, for any
n
, we have
{\langle n,c\rangle}\in{\sf Km}_{0}\setminus\mathcal{W}
or
{\langle n,c\rangle}\in{\sf Km}_{1}\cap\mathcal{W}
.

Proof.

We have:

\displaystyle{\langle n,c\rangle}\not\in\mathcal{W}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle c\cdot{\langle n,c\rangle}\simeq 0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle{\langle n,c\rangle}\in{\sf Km}_{0}

\displaystyle{\langle n,c\rangle}\in\mathcal{W}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle c\cdot{\langle n,c\rangle}\simeq 1

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle{\langle n,c\rangle}\in{\sf Km}_{1}

❑

Lemma 7.2
.

Suppose
\mathcal{Z}
is an RE set such that, for every
m
, there is an
n
such that
{\langle n,m\rangle}\in\mathcal{Z}
.
Then
{\sf Km}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}
and
{\sf Km}_{1}\cap\mathcal{Z}
are effectively inseparable.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathcal{W}
with index
i
is decidable and that
\mathcal{W}
separates
{\sf Km}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}
and
{\sf Km}_{1}\cap\mathcal{Z}
.
Let
\varTheta
and
c
be as in Lemma
7.1
. We find
n
such that
{\langle n,c\rangle}\in\mathcal{Z}
. By Lemma
7.1
, we have

{\langle n,c\rangle}\in{\sf Km}_{0}\setminus\mathcal{W}
or
{\langle n,c\rangle}\in{\sf Km}_{1}\cap\mathcal{W}
. In the first case
{\langle n,c\rangle}\in({\sf Km}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z})\setminus\mathcal{W}
.

Quod non
, by our assumption that
\mathcal{W}
separates
{\sf Km}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}
and
{\sf Km}_{1}\cap\mathcal{Z}
. In the second case, we have

{\langle n,c\rangle}\in{\sf Km}_{1}\cap\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{W}
, again contradicting the assumption.
❑

Theorem 7.2
.

Consider any essentially undecidable RE theory
U
. Then, we can effectively find
(
an index of
)
an
essentially hereditarily undecidable RE theory

V
such that
V\mathrel{\not\!\rhd}U
. Moreover, we can take
V
to be effectively inseparable.

Proof.

Let
T:={\sf Jan}+\{{\sf A}_{n}\mid n\in{\sf Km}_{0}\}
. Let
s
be an index of
T
. We take
A:={\sf pere}(s)
. So,
A
is finitely axiomatised and
recursively boolean isomorphic to
T
. Let
\varPhi
be the witnessing recursive isomorphism from
V
to
A
and let
{\sf B}_{i}:=\varPhi({\sf A}_{i})
.
Clearly, over
A
, every sentence is provably equivalent to a boolean combination of the
{\sf B}_{i}
.

Let
{\sf C}_{n,0},\dots,{\sf C}_{n,2^{n}-1}
be an enumeration of all conjunctions
of
\pm{\sf B}_{i}
, for
i<n
.
Suppose
U
is an essentially undecidable RE theory.
Let
\upsilon_{0},\upsilon_{1},\dots
be an effective enumeration of the theorems
of
U
. Let
\tau_{0},\tau_{1},\dots
be an effective enumeration of all translations from
the
U
-language into the
A
-language.
16
16

16

We will do the argument for the case
of parameter-free translations. By a minor adaptation, we can add parameters.

Consider
n
,
\tau_{i}
and
{\sf C}_{n,j}
, for
j<2^{n}
. Let
{\sf V}_{n,j}:=A+{\sf C}_{n,j}+\{{\sf B}_{k}\mid k\geq n\}
. Clearly,
{\sf V}_{n,j}
is
either inconsistent or consistent and complete.
We claim that, for some
k
, we have
{\sf V}_{n,j}\vdash\neg\,\upsilon^{\tau_{i}}_{k}
.
Suppose this were not the case. Then,
{\sf V}_{n,j}
is consistent and

\tau_{i}
carries an interpretation of
U
in
{\sf V}_{n,j}
, but this is impossible since

{\sf V}_{n,j}
is decidable and
U
is essentially undecidable.

Thus, we can effectively find a number
p_{n,i,j}
as follows.
We find the first
k
such that
{\sf V}_{n,j}\vdash\neg\,\upsilon^{\tau_{i}}_{k}
.
Then, we reduce, the sentence
\upsilon^{\tau_{i}}_{k}
to a boolean combination of

{\sf B}_{s}
over
A
.
Let
p_{n,i,j}
the smallest number of the form
{\langle r,n\rangle}
that is strictly larger
than the
s
such that
{\sf B}_{s}
occurs in this boolean combination.

We define
\eta(n,i)
to be the maximum of the
p_{n,i,j}
, for
j<2^{n}
.
Let
\varPsi(0):=0
and let
\varPsi(k+1):=\eta(\varPsi(k),k)
. Clearly,
\varPsi
is recursive and strictly increasing.
Let
\mathcal{Z}
be the range of
\varPsi
. The set
\mathcal{Z}
is obviously recursive.

We define
V:=A+\{\neg\,{\sf B}_{i}\mid i\in{\sf Km}_{1}\cap\mathcal{Z}\}
.
Suppose, to obtain a contradiction, that we have
K:V\rhd U
.
Let the underlying translation of
K
be
\tau_{n^{\ast}}
.

Clearly, there is a
j^{\ast}
such that

V+{\sf C}_{\varPsi(n^{\ast}),j^{\ast}}
is consistent. (This is a non-constructive step.)
By construction, there is a

\varphi
with
U\vdash\varphi
and
{\sf V}_{\varPsi(n^{\ast}),j^{\ast}}\vdash\neg\,\varphi^{K}
. Moreover,

A\vdash\varphi^{K}\leftrightarrow\rho
, where
\rho
is a
boolean combination of
{\sf B}_{s}
with
s<\varPsi(n^{\ast})
.
We note that no
\neg\,{\sf B}_{r}
with
\varPsi(n^{\ast})<r<\varPsi(n^{\ast}+1)
occurs in the axiomatisation of
V
.
By our assumption on
K
, we have
V\vdash\varphi^{K}
and, so,
V\vdash\rho
. It follows that

A+\{\neg\,{\sf B}_{i}\mid i\in{\sf Km}_{1}\cap\mathcal{Z}\text{ and }i<\varPsi(n^{\ast}+1)\}\vdash\rho.

Hence, also
{\sf V}_{\varPsi(n^{\ast}),j^{\ast}}\vdash\rho
, i.e.,
{\sf V}_{\varPsi(n^{\ast}),j^{\ast}}\vdash\varphi^{K}
,
A contradiction.

We verify that
V
is essentially hereditarily undecidable and effectively inseparable.
We note that
\varXi
with
\varXi(n):={\sf B}_{n}
maps
{\sf Km}_{0}
into
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
and

{\sf Km}_{1}\cap\mathcal{Z}
into
V_{\mathfrak{r}}
. It is immediate from Lemma
7.2

that
{\sf Km}_{0}
and
{\sf Km}_{1}\cap\mathcal{Z}
are effectively inseparable, hence so is

V
. Finally, by Lemma
3.2
, we find that
V
is essentially hereditarily undecidable.
❑

Since the essentially hereditarily undecidable RE theories are closed under interpretability infima,
we again obtain Theorem
7.1
from Theorem
7.2
.

Remark 7.3
.

Yong Cheng notes that the argument of
[
PMV22
, Section 4.2]
yields more information
concerning the possible classes for which the no minimality result holds. See
[
Che22
]
.
His insights can be applied to our case.
For example, we find that Theorem
7.2
tells us that there
is no interpretability minimal element among essentially hereditarily undecidable theories that is
also effectively inseparable.
❍

Appendix A
Parametrically Local Interpretability

In this appendix, we present a reduction notion, to wit
c-lax interpretability
, that improves upon both
lax interpretability and parametrically local interpretability w.r.t. the treatment of essential hereditary undecidability.

A.1.

\pi

0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}}
and use the equivalence between axioms- and theorems-interpretability
to obtain (a) and (b) for
\pi^{\ast}
.

We note that, if we start with a parameter-domain
\pi
that satisfies (a) and (b), then
\pi^{\ast}
is an extension of
\pi
.

A.2.
Parametric Local Interpretability

We say that
V
parametrically locally interprets
U
, or
V\rhd_{\sf pl}U
, iff, there is a parametric translation
\tau
of the
U
-language to the

V
-language, such that, whenever
U\vdash\varphi
, then
V\vdash\exists\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}\;\varphi^{\tau,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}}
.
It is easy to see that
\rhd_{\sf pl}
is transitive, using the known properties of parametric interpretability.

Theorem A.1
.

Let
U
be a theory in a language
\mathcal{L}
extended with constants

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle c\hfil

.
Let
U^{\ast}
be the theory axiomatised by the
\mathcal{L}
-consequences of
U
.
Then,
U\bowtie_{\sf pl}U^{\ast}
.

Proof.

We interpret
U
in
U^{\ast}
via
\tau
which is the identical translation on the vocabulary of
\mathcal{L}
and
which interprets constant
c_{i}
as parameter
p_{i}
.
❑

A.3.
c-Essential Undecidability

Consider a theory
U
. We write
U^{(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern for the theory
U
with its signature expanded with constants

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

.
Tarski in
[
TMR53
]
calls this
an inessential extension
.

We write

for: for some

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle c\hfil$\crcr}}})}\subseteq_{\sf e}V
.
We use
c-essentially
in the obvious way.

Theorem A.2
.

a.

U
is c-essentially undecidable iff
U
is essentially undecidable.

b.

U
is c-essentially hereditarily undecidable iff
U
is essentially hereditarily undecidable.

Proof.

We prove
(
a
)
.

The left-to-right direction is trivial. We treat right-to-left. Suppose
U
is essentially undecidable.
Consider a consistent
U^{\prime}\supseteq_{\sf c}U
.
Suppose
U^{\prime}
is decidable. Let
U^{\ast}
be the theory given by the
U^{\prime}
-theorems in the
U
-language.
Then,
U^{\ast}\supseteq_{\sf e}U
and
U^{\ast}
is consistent and decidable.
Quod impossibile
.

We prove
(
b
)
.
The left-to-right direction is trivial. We treat right-to-left. Suppose
U
is essentially hereditarily undecidable.
Suppose
U^{\prime}\supseteq_{\sf c}U
and
W\subseteq_{\sf e}U^{\prime}
. Let
{U^{\prime}}^{\ast}
be the set of consequences of
U^{\prime}
in
the
U
-language and let
W^{\ast}
be the set of consequences of
W
in the
U
-language.
Then
U\subseteq_{\sf e}{U^{\prime}}^{\ast}
and
W^{\ast}\subseteq_{\sf e}{U^{\prime}}^{\ast}
. If
W
would be decidable, then
so would be
W^{\ast}
.
Quod impossibile
.
❑

The next theorem combines Theorems 4 and 5 of
[
TMR53
, Part I]
.

❑

A.4.
c-Lax Interpretability

We define
V
c-laxly interprets
U
, or
V\mathrel{\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\blacktriangleright$}}}_{\sf c}U
, iff, for all consistent
V^{\prime}\supseteq_{\sf c}V
, there is a consistent

V^{\prime\prime}\supseteq_{\sf c}V^{\prime}
, such that
V^{\prime\prime}\rhd U
.

Theorem A.4
.

V\mathrel{\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\blacktriangleright$}}}_{\sf c}U
iff, for all
V^{\prime}\supseteq_{\sf e}V
, there is a
V^{\prime\prime}\supseteq_{\sf c}V
, such that
V^{\prime\prime}\rhd U
.

Proof.

Left-to-right is trivial. We treat right-to-left. We assume the right-hand-side of our equivalence.
Suppose
V^{\prime}\supseteq_{\sf c}V
and
V^{\prime}
is consistent. Let

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle c\hfil

be the relevant new constants.
We define
{V^{\prime}}^{\ast}
as the theory given by the consequences of
V^{\prime}
in the
V
-language.
Let
Z\supseteq_{\sf c}{V^{\prime}}^{\ast}
be a consistent theory, such that
Z\rhd U
.
Clearly, we may choose the new constants of
Z
, say
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle d\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle d\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle d\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern distinct from

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle c\hfil

.
We take
V^{\prime\prime}:={V^{\prime}}^{(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern .
Clearly,
V^{\prime\prime}\supseteq_{\sf c}V^{\prime}
and

.

V^{\prime\prime}
is consistent. If not, for some

We prove that essential hereditary undecidability is preserved over c-lax interpretability.

Theorem A.5
.

Suppose
V\mathrel{\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\blacktriangleright$}}}_{\sf c}U
and
U
is essentially hereditarily undecidable. Then
V
is essentially hereditarily undecidable.

Proof.

Suppose
V\mathrel{\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\blacktriangleright$}}}_{\sf c}U
and
U
is essentially hereditarily undecidable.
Suppose
W
is a decidable theory in the same language as
V
that is consistent with
V
.
We have
V\subseteq_{\sf e}V\cup W=:V^{\prime}
.
So, there is a consistent
V^{\prime\prime}\supseteq_{\sf c}V^{\prime}
such that
V^{\prime\prime}\rhd U
. Say the witnessing constants are
\vec{c}
and the witnessing
translation is
\tau
. Let
Z:=W^{(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle c\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle c\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle c\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle c\hfil$\crcr}}})}+\mathfrak{id}^{\tau}
. Then, by Theorem
A.3
,
Z
decidable.
Since
Z\subseteq_{\sf e}V^{\prime\prime}
,
so
T:=\{\varphi\mid Z\vdash\varphi^{\tau}\}
is consistent with
U
. Since
Z
is decidable
T
is decidable.
Quod non
.
❑

Theorem A.6
.

Suppose
V\rhd_{\sf pl}U
.
Then,
V\mathrel{\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\blacktriangleright$}}}_{\sf c}U
.

Proof.

Suppose
V\rhd_{\sf pl}U
. Let
\tau
be the witnessing translation.
Consider any consistent
V^{\prime}\supseteq_{\sf e}V
.
We find that
V^{\prime}
proves
\exists\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern , for all

\varphi
such that
U\vdash\varphi
. We take a sequence of fresh constants

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle c\hfil

of the length of

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle p\hfil

. Then, by compactness,

V^{\prime\prime}:=V^{\prime}+\{\varphi^{\tau,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle c\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle c\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle c\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle c\hfil$\crcr}}}}\mid U\vdash\varphi\}
is consistent and, clearly,

V^{\prime\prime}\supseteq_{\sf c}V^{\prime}
and
V^{\prime\prime}\rhd U
.
❑

Appendix B
An Alternative Proof of Theorem
5.5

We show that if
U
is
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-representative, then it is effectively inseparable.
Let
\varPhi
witness that
U
is
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-representative.
Suppose
\mathcal{X}_{0}
and
\mathcal{X}_{1}
are disjoint RE sets.
Via a bijective Gödel numbering, we can view these as
sets of
U
-sentences. Suppose that

U_{\mathfrak{p}}\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{0}
and
U_{\mathfrak{r}}\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{1}
.

Suppose
\exists u\,\delta_{i}(u,x)
represents
\varPhi(x)\in\mathcal{X}_{i}
where
\delta_{i}
is a pure
\Delta_{0}
-formula.

We need a special form of the Gödel fixed point construction. We substitute a formula
\rho
in
\sigma_{0}(v)<\sigma_{1}(v)
in the
following way. First we substitute the numeral of the Gödel number of
\rho
in each of the
\sigma_{i}
.
Then, we rewrite each of the resulting formulas to pure 1-
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-form and, subsequently, we apply witness comparison.
Suppose
Sub
is this function.
Let
{\sf sub}(x,y,z)
be a pure 1-
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-representation of
the graph of
Sub
.
Say
{\sf sub}(x,y,z)
is
\exists w\,\nu(w,x,y,z)
, where
\nu
is pure
\Delta_{0}
and
the witness
w
will always majorise
x
,
y
and
z
.
We consider the formula
\varphi(v)
:

(\exists a\,\exists w\mathbin{<}a\,\exists z\mathbin{<}a\,(\nu(w,v,v,z)\wedge\exists u\mathbin{<}a\,\delta_{1}(u,z)))\;<\\
(\exists b\,\exists w\mathbin{<}a\,\exists z\mathbin{<}b\,(\nu(w,v,v,z)\wedge\exists u\mathbin{<}b\,\delta_{0}(u,z))).

Let
\rho:={\sf Sub}(\varphi,\varphi)
. We note that
\rho
is of the literal form
\rho_{1}<\rho_{0}
, where the
\rho_{i}
are
pure 1-
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-sentences.

Suppose
\varPhi(\rho)\in\mathcal{X}_{i}
. Then
\rho_{i}
. So either
\rho
or
\rho^{\bot}
.
If we have
\rho
, then
\varPhi(\rho)\in\mathcal{X}_{1}
. Moreover, by
\Sigma 1
, we find
U\vdash\varPhi(\rho)
, and, hence,

\varPhi(\rho)\in\mathcal{X}_{0}
.
Quod impossibile
. If we have
\rho^{\bot}
, then

\varPhi(\rho)\in\mathcal{X}_{0}
. Moreover,
by
\Sigma 3
, we have
U\vdash\neg\,\varPhi(\rho)
and, hence,
\varPhi(\rho)\in\mathcal{X}_{1}
.

Quod impossibile iterum
.

Ergo
, the formula
\varPhi(\rho)
is in none of the
\mathcal{X}_{i}
.

Remark B.1
.

Inspection of the proof shows that in order to derive Rosser’s theorem we do not need the formalisation of the Fixed Point Lemma inside the given theory.
❍

Remark B.2
.

The above proof can be somewhat simplified by employing a self-referential Gödel numbering. See, e.g.,
[
Kri23
]
or
[
GV23
]
.
However, even with this strategy, there will be some detail on how to handle substitution of numerals.
❍
